From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating, especially at night. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I go way past what I’d call a normal plate of food. I’ll start with leftovers or whatever’s there, and then it turns into fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—like full meal after full meal—and if there are event leftovers or trays of food in the fridge, I can keep going until a whole tray is gone or it feels like I’ve eaten through half the fridge. It’s been hard to admit out loud, but that’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because before I take that first bite, there’s still this tiny little window where I’m thinking, don’t do this, just go back to bed, just close the fridge. But once I start, it feels like the decision has already been made for me somehow.\n\nI’ll be standing there actually saying in my head, George, stop now, you’ve had enough, this is ridiculous, and at the same time my hands are still fixing another plate or opening another container. It’s like my mind is arguing one thing and my body is doing another. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which almost makes it worse, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I know. I just can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these late-night eating episodes, once you've stopped eating?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I feel… awful. Not just full, I mean sick with myself. I’ll be sitting there looking at the containers or the plates or whatever mess is left, and it’s like this wave of shame hits me. Like, here I am, a grown man, and everybody who knows me probably sees me as organized, dependable, strong, like I’ve got things handled. And then I’m alone in the kitchen at night feeling like I can’t even handle myself.\n\nI feel guilty, embarrassed, disgusted, really. I’ll think, how did I let this happen again? I just told myself I wasn’t going to do it, and then there I am, having done it anyway. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it makes me feel almost like I’m living two different versions of myself, and afterward I really hate that part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. I’m not sitting down and enjoying it or tasting it the way I would if I’d cooked something for dinner.\n\nDuring those episodes I eat fast—really fast. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down, almost like I’m racing something, even though nobody’s there rushing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these eating episodes the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Honestly, the next day it’s hard not to think about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this immediate feeling of, oh God, I did it again. Sometimes before I even fully remember everything, I already feel heavy with it, if that makes sense. Then I start replaying it—what I ate, how much, how fast, the moment I could’ve stopped and didn’t. And I just feel sick with myself.\n\nIt’s not just regret like, oh, I shouldn’t have had that. It’s more personal than that. I start thinking, everybody sees me as this organized, dependable guy, like I’m strong and have myself together, and then there’s this other version of me sitting there alone, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again. That’s the part that really sticks with me the next day. It’s like I’m carrying this secret around, and even if nobody else knows, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, no. That’s part of what makes it feel so… strange, I guess. It’s not like I’m waking up starving or like I skipped dinner and my body is demanding food.\n\nSometimes I’ve literally just eaten dinner with my wife a couple hours before. Like a normal dinner, sitting at the table, everything’s fine. And then later, after things quiet down, I’ll go back into the kitchen and maybe I tell myself I’m just going to put something away or have one bite of leftovers. I’m not really hungry, but I start picking at it anyway.\n\nThen it kind of builds. One bite turns into a few more, and then I’m pulling out other things, making this whole spread almost, even though there wasn’t any real hunger there at the start. It’s hard to admit because it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, like why keep eating if you’re not hungry? But in the moment it doesn’t seem to matter whether I’m hungry or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the loss of control during these episodes is a major part of what leads to your feelings of shame and guilt afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the biggest part of it, honestly. It’s not just the amount of food afterward, although that’s upsetting too. It’s that feeling that I wasn’t really in charge of myself.\n\nOnce I take that first bite, it can feel like the decision has already been made somehow. Like I’ve crossed this line, and even while part of me is still there saying, George, stop now, just stop, put it away, go upstairs… my hands are still doing the opposite. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I wasn’t unaware. I wasn’t confused. I was telling myself not to keep going, and I kept going anyway. So afterward it feels like… what is wrong with me that I couldn’t just stop my own hands from doing it? That loss of control is the part that really eats at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. But I don’t know that I can say that.\n\nSometimes I probably am stressed in that general background way, you know, like just worn down or keyed up, but I don’t always notice some big feeling right before it happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, I’m upset, I need food. A lot of times it’s quieter than that. The house is quiet, the day is basically over, and I just sort of end up in the kitchen. Then afterward I look back and try to assign a reason to it, like maybe I was tense, maybe I was bored, maybe I was avoiding something, but in the moment I don’t always have a clear emotional trigger I can point to.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say no, never, but I also can’t honestly say I usually feel stressed or anxious right before. It’s kind of murky.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with emotions or feelings at those late-night moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, part of me wants to say yes because that seems like the obvious answer, right? Like, why else would I be doing it if I’m not hungry? It feels like it must be serving some purpose, some kind of numbing or calming or… I don’t know, filling up space.\n\nBut at the same time, in the actual moment, I’m not always aware of thinking, I feel bad, so I’m going to eat. It’s usually not that clear. It’s more like I’m restless or the night feels too quiet, or I’m just kind of drifting around, and then I end up eating. And once I start, then maybe it does feel like everything narrows down to the food for a while, like I don’t have to think about anything else. But I don’t know if that means I’m coping with emotions, exactly, or if that’s just me trying to explain it afterward.\n\nSo… maybe. I haven’t really been able to pin it down. I know it does something for me in those moments, because otherwise I don’t think I’d keep ending up there. But I can’t honestly tell you, “yes, I feel sad or anxious and then I eat to handle it,” because it doesn’t feel that straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief or calmness during or immediately after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief exactly. Maybe there’s something like… a narrowing, if that makes sense. While it’s happening, I’m not calm in the way I think of being calm. I’m not peaceful. It’s more like everything gets very focused on the next bite, the next thing, what’s still there, what else I can have. So there may be a break from whatever else was in my head, but it doesn’t feel like healthy relief.\n\nImmediately after, no, not really. If anything, that’s when it starts to feel worse, because the spell breaks a little and I realize what just happened. Sometimes there might be this very brief flat feeling, like I’m full and kind of stunned, and for a minute I’m not thinking clearly. But I wouldn’t say I feel better afterward. It’s more like the noise changes. During it, the noise is all about eating, and afterward the noise is about, why did I do that, why didn’t I stop, how did this happen again.\n\nSo… I guess there may be a temporary shutting-off during it, but I don’t really experience it as calmness. Not in any lasting way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria during these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it euphoria. That word feels too strong for what it is. Like, I don’t feel happy exactly, or joyful, or like I’m having some amazing experience. It’s not like that.\n\nThere can be pleasure in the food, I guess. I mean, I’m not going to pretend it tastes bad in the moment. Sometimes the first few bites especially are kind of… satisfying, or intense, maybe. Like there’s this sense of, okay, this is what I wanted. But it doesn’t stay clean like that, if that makes sense. It turns pretty quickly into something more automatic and urgent, where I’m not really enjoying it in a normal way. I’m just continuing.\n\nSo I’m not sure. There’s probably some pleasure mixed in at the start, but euphoria sounds like I’m feeling great, and I don’t think I am. It’s more like I’m pulled into it, and the food has a strong hold on my attention for a while. Then afterward, whatever pleasure there was feels kind of hard to even remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty before you start eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Before I start, I don’t know that I feel guilt in a clear way. It’s not usually like I’m standing there thinking, “I shouldn’t do this, this is bad,” and feeling guilty already. Sometimes there’s maybe a kind of warning feeling, like I know where it might go if I start, but it’s vague. It’s more like tension or hesitation than guilt.\n\nA lot of the time it feels like I’m negotiating with myself, but not even in full sentences. Like, “I’ll just have a little,” or “this isn’t a big deal,” or I kind of avoid looking directly at what I’m doing. So if there is guilt before, it’s sort of pushed off to the side. I don’t really let myself feel it yet, if that makes sense.\n\nThe guilt is more obvious later. Beforehand it’s muddier. I might know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m probably going to regret it, but in that moment I don’t think I’m fully feeling guilty yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it seems like there should be a clear pattern, like I should be able to say, “Oh, it happens when I feel this,” but I don’t know that I can point to one specific emotion and say that’s the trigger.\n\nSometimes I only realize afterward that I must have been feeling something, if that makes sense. In the moment, it’s not always obvious to me. It’s more like I’m just… in that mode, or moving toward the kitchen, or thinking about food, and I’m not necessarily stopping to identify what I’m feeling first. I don’t usually have a neat sequence in my head of, “I feel upset, therefore I’m going to eat.” It’s muddier than that.\n\nI guess there are times when I can look back and wonder if I was tense or bothered or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but I don’t know if that’s a real trigger or me trying to make sense of it after the fact. I haven’t noticed one consistent emotion that sets it off every time. It feels less like a single clear cause and more like something I slide into before I’ve really named what’s going on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Binge-Eating Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: Once I take that first bite, it feels like the decision has already been made for me, and even while I’m telling myself, 'George, stop now,' my hands keep fixing another plate or opening another container.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I eat so fast during those times that I barely taste food I used to take pride in cooking, and sometimes I’m standing at the counter swallowing big bites before I’ve even finished chewing the last one.\n- key: S053\n  name: Eating_Without_Hunger\n  description: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n  manifestation: A lot of the time I’m not hungry at all—I may have just eaten dinner with my wife—but later I still go back and start picking at leftovers until it turns into another full-on spread.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel sick with myself, like I’m this man everybody sees as organized and strong, but I’m sitting there ashamed, guilty, and disgusted that I let it happen again.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For months now, I can’t think of a single week where it didn’t happen planning meetings and helping with events because I feel embarrassed, drained, and afraid people will notice I’m not holding things together the way I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the foreclosure process started, about a year and two months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria during these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it euphoria. That word feels too strong for what it is. Like, I don’t feel happy exactly, or joyful, or like I’m having some amazing experience. It’s not like that.\n\nThere can be pleasure in the food, I guess. I mean, I’m not going to pretend it tastes bad in the moment. Sometimes the first few bites especially are kind of… satisfying, or intense, maybe. Like there’s this sense of, okay, this is what I wanted. But it doesn’t stay clean like that, if that makes sense. It turns pretty quickly into something more automatic and urgent, where I’m not really enjoying it in a normal way. I’m just continuing.\n\nSo I’m not sure. There’s probably some pleasure mixed in at the start, but euphoria sounds like I’m feeling great, and I don’t think I am. It’s more like I’m pulled into it, and the food has a strong hold on my attention for a while. Then afterward, whatever pleasure there was feels kind of hard to even remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty before you start eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Before I start, I don’t know that I feel guilt in a clear way. It’s not usually like I’m standing there thinking, “I shouldn’t do this, this is bad,” and feeling guilty already. Sometimes there’s maybe a kind of warning feeling, like I know where it might go if I start, but it’s vague. It’s more like tension or hesitation than guilt.\n\nA lot of the time it feels like I’m negotiating with myself, but not even in full sentences. Like, “I’ll just have a little,” or “this isn’t a big deal,” or I kind of avoid looking directly at what I’m doing. So if there is guilt before, it’s sort of pushed off to the side. I don’t really let myself feel it yet, if that makes sense.\n\nThe guilt is more obvious later. Beforehand it’s muddier. I might know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m probably going to regret it, but in that moment I don’t think I’m fully feeling guilty yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it seems like there should be a clear pattern, like I should be able to say, “Oh, it happens when I feel this,” but I don’t know that I can point to one specific emotion and say that’s the trigger.\n\nSometimes I only realize afterward that I must have been feeling something, if that makes sense. In the moment, it’s not always obvious to me. It’s more like I’m just… in that mode, or moving toward the kitchen, or thinking about food, and I’m not necessarily stopping to identify what I’m feeling first. I don’t usually have a neat sequence in my head of, “I feel upset, therefore I’m going to eat.” It’s muddier than that.\n\nI guess there are times when I can look back and wonder if I was tense or bothered or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but I don’t know if that’s a real trigger or me trying to make sense of it after the fact. I haven’t noticed one consistent emotion that sets it off every time. It feels less like a single clear cause and more like something I slide into before I’ve really named what’s going on.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like eating at night helps you escape or get away from your thoughts or feelings?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? But I don’t experience it in a really clear way, like “I’m eating so I don’t have to think about something.” It’s not that organized in my head.

There are times when, once I start eating, my attention narrows a lot. Like I’m focused on the food, what I’m going to have next, whether there’s more of it, that kind of thing. So in that sense, maybe it does push everything else out for a while. But I don’t always know what it’s pushing out, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking about a specific problem and then consciously using food to escape it.

It’s more like everything gets quieter, or at least less specific. But I don’t know if that’s escape, or distraction, or just getting caught up in the eating itself. I hesitate to say yes too strongly because I don’t want to make it sound like I fully understand what I’m doing in the moment. A lot of the time I only try to make sense of it afterward.

That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. I’m not sitting down and enjoying it or tasting it the way I would if I’d cooked something for dinner.\n\nDuring those episodes I eat fast—really fast. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. I start thinking, everybody sees me as this organized, dependable guy, like I’m strong and have myself together, and then there’s this other version of me sitting there alone, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again. That’s the part that really sticks with me the next day. Like a normal dinner, sitting at the table, everything’s fine. I’m not really hungry, but I start picking at it anyway.\n\nThen it kind of builds. One bite turns into a few more, and then I’m pulling out other things, making this whole spread almost, even though there wasn’t any real hunger there at the start. It’s hard to admit because it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, like why keep eating if you’re not hungry? But in the moment it doesn’t seem to matter whether I’m hungry or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the loss of control during these episodes is a major part of what leads to your feelings of shame and guilt afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the biggest part of it, honestly. It’s not just the amount of food afterward, although that’s upsetting too. It’s that feeling that I wasn’t really in charge of myself.\n\nOnce I take that first bite, it can feel like the decision has already been made somehow. Like I’ve crossed this line, and even while part of me is still there saying, George, stop now, just stop, put it away, go upstairs… my hands are still doing the opposite. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating, especially at night. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I go way past what I’d call a normal plate of food. I’ll start with leftovers or whatever’s there, and then it turns into fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—like full meal after full meal—and if there are event leftovers or trays of food in the fridge, I can keep going until a whole tray is gone or it feels like I’ve eaten through half the fridge. It’s been hard to admit out loud, but that’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because before I take that first bite, there’s still this tiny little window where I’m thinking, don’t do this, just go back to bed, just close the fridge. But once I start, it feels like the decision has already been made for me somehow.\n\nI’ll be standing there actually saying in my head, George, stop now, you’ve had enough, this is ridiculous, and at the same time my hands are still fixing another plate or opening another container. It’s like my mind is arguing one thing and my body is doing another. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which almost makes it worse, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I know. Not just full, I mean sick with myself. I’ll be sitting there looking at the containers or the plates or whatever mess is left, and it’s like this wave of shame hits me. Like, here I am, a grown man, and everybody who knows me probably sees me as organized, dependable, strong, like I’ve got things handled. And then I’m alone in the kitchen at night feeling like I can’t even handle myself.\n\nI feel guilty, embarrassed, disgusted, really. I’ll think, how did I let this happen again? I just told myself I wasn’t going to do it, and then there I am, having done it anyway. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it makes me feel almost like I’m living two different versions of myself, and afterward I really hate that part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. I’m not sitting down and enjoying it or tasting it the way I would if I’d cooked something for dinner.\n\nDuring those episodes I eat fast—really fast. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down, almost like I’m racing something, even though nobody’s there rushing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these eating episodes the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Honestly, the next day it’s hard not to think about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this immediate feeling of, oh God, I did it again. Sometimes before I even fully remember everything, I already feel heavy with it, if that makes sense. Then I start replaying it—what I ate, how much, how fast, the moment I could’ve stopped and didn’t. And I just feel sick with myself.\n\nIt’s not just regret like, oh, I shouldn’t have had that. It’s more personal than that. I start thinking, everybody sees me as this organized, dependable guy, like I’m strong and have myself together, and then there’s this other version of me sitting there alone, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again. That’s the part that really sticks with me the next day. It’s like I’m carrying this secret around, and even if nobody else knows, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, no. That’s part of what makes it feel so… strange, I guess. It’s not like I’m waking up starving or like I skipped dinner and my body is demanding food.\n\nSometimes I’ve literally just eaten dinner with my wife a couple hours before. Like a normal dinner, sitting at the table, everything’s fine. And then later, after things quiet down, I’ll go back into the kitchen and maybe I tell myself I’m just going to put something away or have one bite of leftovers. I’m not really hungry, but I start picking at it anyway.\n\nThen it kind of builds. One bite turns into a few more, and then I’m pulling out other things, making this whole spread almost, even though there wasn’t any real hunger there at the start. It’s hard to admit because it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, like why keep eating if you’re not hungry? But in the moment it doesn’t seem to matter whether I’m hungry or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the loss of control during these episodes is a major part of what leads to your feelings of shame and guilt afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the biggest part of it, honestly. It’s not just the amount of food afterward, although that’s upsetting too. It’s that feeling that I wasn’t really in charge of myself.\n\nOnce I take that first bite, it can feel like the decision has already been made somehow. Like I’ve crossed this line, and even while part of me is still there saying, George, stop now, just stop, put it away, go upstairs… my hands are still doing the opposite. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I wasn’t unaware. I wasn’t confused. I was telling myself not to keep going, and I kept going anyway. So afterward it feels like… what is wrong with me that I couldn’t just stop my own hands from doing it? That loss of control is the part that really eats at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. But I don’t know that I can say that.\n\nSometimes I probably am stressed in that general background way, you know, like just worn down or keyed up, but I don’t always notice some big feeling right before it happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, I’m upset, I need food. A lot of times it’s quieter than that. The house is quiet, the day is basically over, and I just sort of end up in the kitchen. Then afterward I look back and try to assign a reason to it, like maybe I was tense, maybe I was bored, maybe I was avoiding something, but in the moment I don’t always have a clear emotional trigger I can point to.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say no, never, but I also can’t honestly say I usually feel stressed or anxious right before. It’s kind of murky.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with emotions or feelings at those late-night moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, part of me wants to say yes because that seems like the obvious answer, right? Like, why else would I be doing it if I’m not hungry? It feels like it must be serving some purpose, some kind of numbing or calming or… I don’t know, filling up space.\n\nBut at the same time, in the actual moment, I’m not always aware of thinking, I feel bad, so I’m going to eat. It’s usually not that clear. It’s more like I’m restless or the night feels too quiet, or I’m just kind of drifting around, and then I end up eating. And once I start, then maybe it does feel like everything narrows down to the food for a while, like I don’t have to think about anything else. But I don’t know if that means I’m coping with emotions, exactly, or if that’s just me trying to explain it afterward.\n\nSo… maybe. I haven’t really been able to pin it down. I know it does something for me in those moments, because otherwise I don’t think I’d keep ending up there. But I can’t honestly tell you, “yes, I feel sad or anxious and then I eat to handle it,” because it doesn’t feel that straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief or calmness during or immediately after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief exactly. Maybe there’s something like… a narrowing, if that makes sense. While it’s happening, I’m not calm in the way I think of being calm. I’m not peaceful. It’s more like everything gets very focused on the next bite, the next thing, what’s still there, what else I can have. So there may be a break from whatever else was in my head, but it doesn’t feel like healthy relief.\n\nImmediately after, no, not really. If anything, that’s when it starts to feel worse, because the spell breaks a little and I realize what just happened. Sometimes there might be this very brief flat feeling, like I’m full and kind of stunned, and for a minute I’m not thinking clearly. But I wouldn’t say I feel better afterward. It’s more like the noise changes. During it, the noise is all about eating, and afterward the noise is about, why did I do that, why didn’t I stop, how did this happen again.\n\nSo… I guess there may be a temporary shutting-off during it, but I don’t really experience it as calmness. Not in any lasting way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria during these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it euphoria. That word feels too strong for what it is. Like, I don’t feel happy exactly, or joyful, or like I’m having some amazing experience. It’s not like that.\n\nThere can be pleasure in the food, I guess. I mean, I’m not going to pretend it tastes bad in the moment. Sometimes the first few bites especially are kind of… satisfying, or intense, maybe. Like there’s this sense of, okay, this is what I wanted. But it doesn’t stay clean like that, if that makes sense. It turns pretty quickly into something more automatic and urgent, where I’m not really enjoying it in a normal way. I’m just continuing.\n\nSo I’m not sure. There’s probably some pleasure mixed in at the start, but euphoria sounds like I’m feeling great, and I don’t think I am. It’s more like I’m pulled into it, and the food has a strong hold on my attention for a while. Then afterward, whatever pleasure there was feels kind of hard to even remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty before you start eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Before I start, I don’t know that I feel guilt in a clear way. It’s not usually like I’m standing there thinking, “I shouldn’t do this, this is bad,” and feeling guilty already. Sometimes there’s maybe a kind of warning feeling, like I know where it might go if I start, but it’s vague. It’s more like tension or hesitation than guilt.\n\nA lot of the time it feels like I’m negotiating with myself, but not even in full sentences. Like, “I’ll just have a little,” or “this isn’t a big deal,” or I kind of avoid looking directly at what I’m doing. So if there is guilt before, it’s sort of pushed off to the side. I don’t really let myself feel it yet, if that makes sense.\n\nThe guilt is more obvious later. Beforehand it’s muddier. I might know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m probably going to regret it, but in that moment I don’t think I’m fully feeling guilty yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it seems like there should be a clear pattern, like I should be able to say, “Oh, it happens when I feel this,” but I don’t know that I can point to one specific emotion and say that’s the trigger.\n\nSometimes I only realize afterward that I must have been feeling something, if that makes sense. In the moment, it’s not always obvious to me. It’s more like I’m just… in that mode, or moving toward the kitchen, or thinking about food, and I’m not necessarily stopping to identify what I’m feeling first. I don’t usually have a neat sequence in my head of, “I feel upset, therefore I’m going to eat.” It’s muddier than that.\n\nI guess there are times when I can look back and wonder if I was tense or bothered or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but I don’t know if that’s a real trigger or me trying to make sense of it after the fact. I haven’t noticed one consistent emotion that sets it off every time. It feels less like a single clear cause and more like something I slide into before I’ve really named what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating at night helps you escape or get away from your thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? But I don’t experience it in a really clear way, like “I’m eating so I don’t have to think about something.” It’s not that organized in my head.\n\nThere are times when, once I start eating, my attention narrows a lot. Like I’m focused on the food, what I’m going to have next, whether there’s more of it, that kind of thing. So in that sense, maybe it does push everything else out for a while. But I don’t always know what it’s pushing out, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking about a specific problem and then consciously using food to escape it.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets quieter, or at least less specific. But I don’t know if that’s escape, or distraction, or just getting caught up in the eating itself. I hesitate to say yes too strongly because I don’t want to make it sound like I fully understand what I’m doing in the moment. A lot of the time I only try to make sense of it afterward.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know. I’m hesitant to say yes, because “emotionally overwhelmed” sounds very clear, like I’m aware that I’m flooded with feelings and then I react by eating. I don’t usually recognize it that plainly in the moment.

So… maybe sometimes, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a definite pattern. It feels less obvious than that to me.

---------- turn 15 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating, especially at night. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I go way past what I’d call a normal plate of food. I’ll start with leftovers or whatever’s there, and then it turns into fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—like full meal after full meal—and if there are event leftovers or trays of food in the fridge, I can keep going until a whole tray is gone or it feels like I’ve eaten through half the fridge. It’s been hard to admit out loud, but that’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because before I take that first bite, there’s still this tiny little window where I’m thinking, don’t do this, just go back to bed, just close the fridge. But once I start, it feels like the decision has already been made for me somehow.\n\nI’ll be standing there actually saying in my head, George, stop now, you’ve had enough, this is ridiculous, and at the same time my hands are still fixing another plate or opening another container. It’s like my mind is arguing one thing and my body is doing another. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which almost makes it worse, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I know. I just can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these late-night eating episodes, once you've stopped eating?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I feel… awful. Not just full, I mean sick with myself. I’ll be sitting there looking at the containers or the plates or whatever mess is left, and it’s like this wave of shame hits me. Like, here I am, a grown man, and everybody who knows me probably sees me as organized, dependable, strong, like I’ve got things handled. And then I’m alone in the kitchen at night feeling like I can’t even handle myself.\n\nI feel guilty, embarrassed, disgusted, really. I’ll think, how did I let this happen again? I just told myself I wasn’t going to do it, and then there I am, having done it anyway. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it makes me feel almost like I’m living two different versions of myself, and afterward I really hate that part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. I’m not sitting down and enjoying it or tasting it the way I would if I’d cooked something for dinner.\n\nDuring those episodes I eat fast—really fast. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down, almost like I’m racing something, even though nobody’s there rushing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these eating episodes the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Honestly, the next day it’s hard not to think about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this immediate feeling of, oh God, I did it again. Sometimes before I even fully remember everything, I already feel heavy with it, if that makes sense. Then I start replaying it—what I ate, how much, how fast, the moment I could’ve stopped and didn’t. And I just feel sick with myself.\n\nIt’s not just regret like, oh, I shouldn’t have had that. It’s more personal than that. I start thinking, everybody sees me as this organized, dependable guy, like I’m strong and have myself together, and then there’s this other version of me sitting there alone, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again. That’s the part that really sticks with me the next day. It’s like I’m carrying this secret around, and even if nobody else knows, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, no. That’s part of what makes it feel so… strange, I guess. It’s not like I’m waking up starving or like I skipped dinner and my body is demanding food.\n\nSometimes I’ve literally just eaten dinner with my wife a couple hours before. Like a normal dinner, sitting at the table, everything’s fine. And then later, after things quiet down, I’ll go back into the kitchen and maybe I tell myself I’m just going to put something away or have one bite of leftovers. I’m not really hungry, but I start picking at it anyway.\n\nThen it kind of builds. One bite turns into a few more, and then I’m pulling out other things, making this whole spread almost, even though there wasn’t any real hunger there at the start. It’s hard to admit because it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, like why keep eating if you’re not hungry? But in the moment it doesn’t seem to matter whether I’m hungry or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the loss of control during these episodes is a major part of what leads to your feelings of shame and guilt afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the biggest part of it, honestly. It’s not just the amount of food afterward, although that’s upsetting too. It’s that feeling that I wasn’t really in charge of myself.\n\nOnce I take that first bite, it can feel like the decision has already been made somehow. Like I’ve crossed this line, and even while part of me is still there saying, George, stop now, just stop, put it away, go upstairs… my hands are still doing the opposite. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I wasn’t unaware. I wasn’t confused. I was telling myself not to keep going, and I kept going anyway. So afterward it feels like… what is wrong with me that I couldn’t just stop my own hands from doing it? That loss of control is the part that really eats at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. But I don’t know that I can say that.\n\nSometimes I probably am stressed in that general background way, you know, like just worn down or keyed up, but I don’t always notice some big feeling right before it happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, I’m upset, I need food. A lot of times it’s quieter than that. The house is quiet, the day is basically over, and I just sort of end up in the kitchen. Then afterward I look back and try to assign a reason to it, like maybe I was tense, maybe I was bored, maybe I was avoiding something, but in the moment I don’t always have a clear emotional trigger I can point to.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say no, never, but I also can’t honestly say I usually feel stressed or anxious right before. It’s kind of murky.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with emotions or feelings at those late-night moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, part of me wants to say yes because that seems like the obvious answer, right? Like, why else would I be doing it if I’m not hungry? It feels like it must be serving some purpose, some kind of numbing or calming or… I don’t know, filling up space.\n\nBut at the same time, in the actual moment, I’m not always aware of thinking, I feel bad, so I’m going to eat. It’s usually not that clear. It’s more like I’m restless or the night feels too quiet, or I’m just kind of drifting around, and then I end up eating. And once I start, then maybe it does feel like everything narrows down to the food for a while, like I don’t have to think about anything else. But I don’t know if that means I’m coping with emotions, exactly, or if that’s just me trying to explain it afterward.\n\nSo… maybe. I haven’t really been able to pin it down. I know it does something for me in those moments, because otherwise I don’t think I’d keep ending up there. But I can’t honestly tell you, “yes, I feel sad or anxious and then I eat to handle it,” because it doesn’t feel that straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief or calmness during or immediately after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief exactly. Maybe there’s something like… a narrowing, if that makes sense. While it’s happening, I’m not calm in the way I think of being calm. I’m not peaceful. It’s more like everything gets very focused on the next bite, the next thing, what’s still there, what else I can have. So there may be a break from whatever else was in my head, but it doesn’t feel like healthy relief.\n\nImmediately after, no, not really. If anything, that’s when it starts to feel worse, because the spell breaks a little and I realize what just happened. Sometimes there might be this very brief flat feeling, like I’m full and kind of stunned, and for a minute I’m not thinking clearly. But I wouldn’t say I feel better afterward. It’s more like the noise changes. During it, the noise is all about eating, and afterward the noise is about, why did I do that, why didn’t I stop, how did this happen again.\n\nSo… I guess there may be a temporary shutting-off during it, but I don’t really experience it as calmness. Not in any lasting way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria during these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it euphoria. That word feels too strong for what it is. Like, I don’t feel happy exactly, or joyful, or like I’m having some amazing experience. It’s not like that.\n\nThere can be pleasure in the food, I guess. I mean, I’m not going to pretend it tastes bad in the moment. Sometimes the first few bites especially are kind of… satisfying, or intense, maybe. Like there’s this sense of, okay, this is what I wanted. But it doesn’t stay clean like that, if that makes sense. It turns pretty quickly into something more automatic and urgent, where I’m not really enjoying it in a normal way. I’m just continuing.\n\nSo I’m not sure. There’s probably some pleasure mixed in at the start, but euphoria sounds like I’m feeling great, and I don’t think I am. It’s more like I’m pulled into it, and the food has a strong hold on my attention for a while. Then afterward, whatever pleasure there was feels kind of hard to even remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty before you start eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Before I start, I don’t know that I feel guilt in a clear way. It’s not usually like I’m standing there thinking, “I shouldn’t do this, this is bad,” and feeling guilty already. Sometimes there’s maybe a kind of warning feeling, like I know where it might go if I start, but it’s vague. It’s more like tension or hesitation than guilt.\n\nA lot of the time it feels like I’m negotiating with myself, but not even in full sentences. Like, “I’ll just have a little,” or “this isn’t a big deal,” or I kind of avoid looking directly at what I’m doing. So if there is guilt before, it’s sort of pushed off to the side. I don’t really let myself feel it yet, if that makes sense.\n\nThe guilt is more obvious later. Beforehand it’s muddier. I might know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m probably going to regret it, but in that moment I don’t think I’m fully feeling guilty yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it seems like there should be a clear pattern, like I should be able to say, “Oh, it happens when I feel this,” but I don’t know that I can point to one specific emotion and say that’s the trigger.\n\nSometimes I only realize afterward that I must have been feeling something, if that makes sense. In the moment, it’s not always obvious to me. It’s more like I’m just… in that mode, or moving toward the kitchen, or thinking about food, and I’m not necessarily stopping to identify what I’m feeling first. I don’t usually have a neat sequence in my head of, “I feel upset, therefore I’m going to eat.” It’s muddier than that.\n\nI guess there are times when I can look back and wonder if I was tense or bothered or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but I don’t know if that’s a real trigger or me trying to make sense of it after the fact. I haven’t noticed one consistent emotion that sets it off every time. It feels less like a single clear cause and more like something I slide into before I’ve really named what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating at night helps you escape or get away from your thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? But I don’t experience it in a really clear way, like “I’m eating so I don’t have to think about something.” It’s not that organized in my head.\n\nThere are times when, once I start eating, my attention narrows a lot. Like I’m focused on the food, what I’m going to have next, whether there’s more of it, that kind of thing. So in that sense, maybe it does push everything else out for a while. But I don’t always know what it’s pushing out, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking about a specific problem and then consciously using food to escape it.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets quieter, or at least less specific. But I don’t know if that’s escape, or distraction, or just getting caught up in the eating itself. I hesitate to say yes too strongly because I don’t want to make it sound like I fully understand what I’m doing in the moment. A lot of the time I only try to make sense of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are a reaction to feeling emotionally overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m hesitant to say yes, because “emotionally overwhelmed” sounds very clear, like I’m aware that I’m flooded with feelings and then I react by eating. I don’t usually recognize it that plainly in the moment.\n\nSometimes afterward I can look back and think, maybe I was overwhelmed, or maybe I was carrying more than I realized. But while it’s happening, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t handle these feelings.” It’s more like I just get pulled into the eating, and only later I try to figure out what was underneath it.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a definite pattern. It feels less obvious than that to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is my eating, especially at night. Most nights, after my wife goes to bed, I end up in the kitchen and it’s like I go way past what I’d call a normal plate of food. I’ll start with leftovers or whatever’s there, and then it turns into fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—like full meal after full meal—and if there are event leftovers or trays of food in the fridge, I can keep going until a whole tray is gone or it feels like I’ve eaten through half the fridge. It’s been hard to admit out loud, but that’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… no, I really don’t feel in control once it starts. It’s strange because before I take that first bite, there’s still this tiny little window where I’m thinking, don’t do this, just go back to bed, just close the fridge. But once I start, it feels like the decision has already been made for me somehow.\n\nI’ll be standing there actually saying in my head, George, stop now, you’ve had enough, this is ridiculous, and at the same time my hands are still fixing another plate or opening another container. It’s like my mind is arguing one thing and my body is doing another. I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which almost makes it worse, because it’s not like I don’t know what I’m doing. I know. I just can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these late-night eating episodes, once you've stopped eating?\n\nPatient: Afterward, I feel… awful. Not just full, I mean sick with myself. I’ll be sitting there looking at the containers or the plates or whatever mess is left, and it’s like this wave of shame hits me. Like, here I am, a grown man, and everybody who knows me probably sees me as organized, dependable, strong, like I’ve got things handled. And then I’m alone in the kitchen at night feeling like I can’t even handle myself.\n\nI feel guilty, embarrassed, disgusted, really. I’ll think, how did I let this happen again? I just told myself I wasn’t going to do it, and then there I am, having done it anyway. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it makes me feel almost like I’m living two different versions of myself, and afterward I really hate that part of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. I’m not sitting down and enjoying it or tasting it the way I would if I’d cooked something for dinner.\n\nDuring those episodes I eat fast—really fast. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down, almost like I’m racing something, even though nobody’s there rushing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these eating episodes the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Honestly, the next day it’s hard not to think about it. It kind of follows me around.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this immediate feeling of, oh God, I did it again. Sometimes before I even fully remember everything, I already feel heavy with it, if that makes sense. Then I start replaying it—what I ate, how much, how fast, the moment I could’ve stopped and didn’t. And I just feel sick with myself.\n\nIt’s not just regret like, oh, I shouldn’t have had that. It’s more personal than that. I start thinking, everybody sees me as this organized, dependable guy, like I’m strong and have myself together, and then there’s this other version of me sitting there alone, ashamed and guilty and disgusted that I let it happen again. That’s the part that really sticks with me the next day. It’s like I’m carrying this secret around, and even if nobody else knows, I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel hungry before you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: A lot of the time, no. That’s part of what makes it feel so… strange, I guess. It’s not like I’m waking up starving or like I skipped dinner and my body is demanding food.\n\nSometimes I’ve literally just eaten dinner with my wife a couple hours before. Like a normal dinner, sitting at the table, everything’s fine. And then later, after things quiet down, I’ll go back into the kitchen and maybe I tell myself I’m just going to put something away or have one bite of leftovers. I’m not really hungry, but I start picking at it anyway.\n\nThen it kind of builds. One bite turns into a few more, and then I’m pulling out other things, making this whole spread almost, even though there wasn’t any real hunger there at the start. It’s hard to admit because it sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, like why keep eating if you’re not hungry? But in the moment it doesn’t seem to matter whether I’m hungry or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the loss of control during these episodes is a major part of what leads to your feelings of shame and guilt afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably the biggest part of it, honestly. It’s not just the amount of food afterward, although that’s upsetting too. It’s that feeling that I wasn’t really in charge of myself.\n\nOnce I take that first bite, it can feel like the decision has already been made somehow. Like I’ve crossed this line, and even while part of me is still there saying, George, stop now, just stop, put it away, go upstairs… my hands are still doing the opposite. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I wasn’t unaware. I wasn’t confused. I was telling myself not to keep going, and I kept going anyway. So afterward it feels like… what is wrong with me that I couldn’t just stop my own hands from doing it? That loss of control is the part that really eats at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. But I don’t know that I can say that.\n\nSometimes I probably am stressed in that general background way, you know, like just worn down or keyed up, but I don’t always notice some big feeling right before it happens. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, I’m upset, I need food. A lot of times it’s quieter than that. The house is quiet, the day is basically over, and I just sort of end up in the kitchen. Then afterward I look back and try to assign a reason to it, like maybe I was tense, maybe I was bored, maybe I was avoiding something, but in the moment I don’t always have a clear emotional trigger I can point to.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say no, never, but I also can’t honestly say I usually feel stressed or anxious right before. It’s kind of murky.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with emotions or feelings at those late-night moments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, part of me wants to say yes because that seems like the obvious answer, right? Like, why else would I be doing it if I’m not hungry? It feels like it must be serving some purpose, some kind of numbing or calming or… I don’t know, filling up space.\n\nBut at the same time, in the actual moment, I’m not always aware of thinking, I feel bad, so I’m going to eat. It’s usually not that clear. It’s more like I’m restless or the night feels too quiet, or I’m just kind of drifting around, and then I end up eating. And once I start, then maybe it does feel like everything narrows down to the food for a while, like I don’t have to think about anything else. But I don’t know if that means I’m coping with emotions, exactly, or if that’s just me trying to explain it afterward.\n\nSo… maybe. I haven’t really been able to pin it down. I know it does something for me in those moments, because otherwise I don’t think I’d keep ending up there. But I can’t honestly tell you, “yes, I feel sad or anxious and then I eat to handle it,” because it doesn’t feel that straightforward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any sense of relief or calmness during or immediately after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it relief exactly. Maybe there’s something like… a narrowing, if that makes sense. While it’s happening, I’m not calm in the way I think of being calm. I’m not peaceful. It’s more like everything gets very focused on the next bite, the next thing, what’s still there, what else I can have. So there may be a break from whatever else was in my head, but it doesn’t feel like healthy relief.\n\nImmediately after, no, not really. If anything, that’s when it starts to feel worse, because the spell breaks a little and I realize what just happened. Sometimes there might be this very brief flat feeling, like I’m full and kind of stunned, and for a minute I’m not thinking clearly. But I wouldn’t say I feel better afterward. It’s more like the noise changes. During it, the noise is all about eating, and afterward the noise is about, why did I do that, why didn’t I stop, how did this happen again.\n\nSo… I guess there may be a temporary shutting-off during it, but I don’t really experience it as calmness. Not in any lasting way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel a sense of pleasure or euphoria during these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it euphoria. That word feels too strong for what it is. Like, I don’t feel happy exactly, or joyful, or like I’m having some amazing experience. It’s not like that.\n\nThere can be pleasure in the food, I guess. I mean, I’m not going to pretend it tastes bad in the moment. Sometimes the first few bites especially are kind of… satisfying, or intense, maybe. Like there’s this sense of, okay, this is what I wanted. But it doesn’t stay clean like that, if that makes sense. It turns pretty quickly into something more automatic and urgent, where I’m not really enjoying it in a normal way. I’m just continuing.\n\nSo I’m not sure. There’s probably some pleasure mixed in at the start, but euphoria sounds like I’m feeling great, and I don’t think I am. It’s more like I’m pulled into it, and the food has a strong hold on my attention for a while. Then afterward, whatever pleasure there was feels kind of hard to even remember clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty before you start eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Before I start, I don’t know that I feel guilt in a clear way. It’s not usually like I’m standing there thinking, “I shouldn’t do this, this is bad,” and feeling guilty already. Sometimes there’s maybe a kind of warning feeling, like I know where it might go if I start, but it’s vague. It’s more like tension or hesitation than guilt.\n\nA lot of the time it feels like I’m negotiating with myself, but not even in full sentences. Like, “I’ll just have a little,” or “this isn’t a big deal,” or I kind of avoid looking directly at what I’m doing. So if there is guilt before, it’s sort of pushed off to the side. I don’t really let myself feel it yet, if that makes sense.\n\nThe guilt is more obvious later. Beforehand it’s muddier. I might know, somewhere in the back of my mind, that I’m probably going to regret it, but in that moment I don’t think I’m fully feeling guilty yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it seems like there should be a clear pattern, like I should be able to say, “Oh, it happens when I feel this,” but I don’t know that I can point to one specific emotion and say that’s the trigger.\n\nSometimes I only realize afterward that I must have been feeling something, if that makes sense. In the moment, it’s not always obvious to me. It’s more like I’m just… in that mode, or moving toward the kitchen, or thinking about food, and I’m not necessarily stopping to identify what I’m feeling first. I don’t usually have a neat sequence in my head of, “I feel upset, therefore I’m going to eat.” It’s muddier than that.\n\nI guess there are times when I can look back and wonder if I was tense or bothered or just kind of uncomfortable in myself, but I don’t know if that’s a real trigger or me trying to make sense of it after the fact. I haven’t noticed one consistent emotion that sets it off every time. It feels less like a single clear cause and more like something I slide into before I’ve really named what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating at night helps you escape or get away from your thoughts or feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, maybe? But I don’t experience it in a really clear way, like “I’m eating so I don’t have to think about something.” It’s not that organized in my head.\n\nThere are times when, once I start eating, my attention narrows a lot. Like I’m focused on the food, what I’m going to have next, whether there’s more of it, that kind of thing. So in that sense, maybe it does push everything else out for a while. But I don’t always know what it’s pushing out, if that makes sense. I’m not sitting there thinking about a specific problem and then consciously using food to escape it.\n\nIt’s more like everything gets quieter, or at least less specific. But I don’t know if that’s escape, or distraction, or just getting caught up in the eating itself. I hesitate to say yes too strongly because I don’t want to make it sound like I fully understand what I’m doing in the moment. A lot of the time I only try to make sense of it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are a reaction to feeling emotionally overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I’m hesitant to say yes, because “emotionally overwhelmed” sounds very clear, like I’m aware that I’m flooded with feelings and then I react by eating. I don’t usually recognize it that plainly in the moment.\n\nSometimes afterward I can look back and think, maybe I was overwhelmed, or maybe I was carrying more than I realized. But while it’s happening, I’m not usually thinking, “I can’t handle these feelings.” It’s more like I just get pulled into the eating, and only later I try to figure out what was underneath it.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a definite pattern. It feels less obvious than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unfulfilled or unsatisfied with your daily life or activities outside of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know if I’d say I feel “unfulfilled” exactly, because that sounds like I’ve sat down and compared my life to some standard and decided it’s empty or something. I don’t usually think about it in that clear of a way.\n\nThere are definitely stretches of the day that feel kind of flat, or like I’m just moving through time rather than really getting much out of it, but I don’t know whether that’s the same thing as being unsatisfied with my life or activities. Sometimes I’ll do things and they don’t really hold my attention, and then later I wonder if maybe the eating is filling some kind of gap, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed that as a definite connection.\n\nI guess I’m hesitant to say yes, because I don’t want to make it sound like I have this clear thought of, “My day wasn’t meaningful, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not like that in my head. It’s more vague than that. I don’t always know what I’m feeling about my day until after the fact, if even then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’ll start with leftovers or whatever’s there, and then it turns into fried chicken, mac and cheese, sweet potatoes, rolls—like full meal after full meal—and if there are event leftovers or trays of food in the fridge, I can keep going until a whole tray is gone or it feels like I’ve eaten through half the fridge. That’s actually one of the parts that bothers me when I think about it afterward, because I don’t eat like I normally would. Sometimes I’m standing right there at the counter, not even using a proper plate the whole time, just taking these big bites and swallowing before I’ve even really finished chewing the last bite. And the sad thing is, some of the food is stuff I used to really take pride in making, you know? Like food I would normally want people to enjoy. But in that moment I barely taste it. I’ll be fixing another plate, or opening another container, or going back for something else like I’m on autopilot.\n\nAnd that’s what makes me feel so ashamed later, because I can remember those moments where I knew I wanted to stop. I’ve tried to figure that out, because it would make more sense to me if I could say, yes, I’m always anxious right before, or yes, it only happens after a bad day. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, I’m upset, I need food. A lot of times it’s quieter than that. The house is quiet, the day is basically over, and I just sort of end up in the kitchen. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I’ve wondered that, but I can’t really point to one thing and say, yes, that’s what’s missing and that’s what the food is replacing.

It’s more like… at night, things get quieter, and there’s less structure, and I’m kind of left with myself. Sometimes food becomes the main thing I’m thinking about, but I don’t know if that means I’m trying to fill an emptiness or if it’s just become this pattern I fall into. I don’t want to over-explain it and make it sound more insightful than it is, because in the moment I’m usually not thinking, “Something is missing from my life.” I’m just thinking about eating, or I’m trying not to think about eating, and then it sort of takes over.

Afterward, I can ask myself those kinds of questions, like maybe I’m bored, or lonely, or not getting enough out of the day, but I don’t know that for sure. It feels like I’m guessing after the fact. So I guess my honest answer is: maybe, but I haven’t clearly noticed it as “I’m filling something with food.” It’s much murkier than that for me.
